  • Trap 4: Equating sabk‑i‑hindī with “ornate” or “decadent” Persian poetry; it is a deliberate aesthetic program integrating Indic conceptual resources into Persian forms, recognized as such by contemporary critics like Āzād Bilgrāmī.
  • Trap 5: Reading the Vaiṣṇava Sahajiyā maithuna as mere antinomianism; it is a highly regulated, gnosticized ritual requiring guru authorization, breath discipline, and subtle‑body visualization, documented in manuals like the Prema‑vilāsa.
  • Trap 6: Attributing the Caryāpada exclusively to Buddhism; the verses were preserved and transmitted in Buddhist monasteries but their language (Abahatta) and imagery (the “empty boat,” the “unstruck sound”) were appropriated by later Hindu and Muslim mystics in Bengal.
  • Trap 7: Confusing the Haridāsa movement’s social composition (merchant, shepherd) with the Varkari tradition’s (tailor, potter); both are vernacular bhakti, but Haridāsas explicitly encode Dvaita metaphysics, while Varkaris emphasize non‑sectarian vitthal‑bhakti.

The Samkhya‑Yoga Synthesis: Metaphysics of Prakṛti, Puruṣa, and the Role of the Guṇas

Classical Sāṃkhya, codified in the Sāṃkhya‑kārikā of Īśvarakṛṣṇa (c. 4th century CE), posits a dualistic ontology of two ultimate realities: puruṣa (pure consciousness, plural, inactive) and prakṛti (primordial matter, singular, dynamic). Prakṛti evolves through the interplay of three guṇas — sattva (lucidity), rajas (activity), and tamas (inertia) — producing the twenty‑four tattvas that constitute the manifest world, from mahat (buddhi) down to the five gross elements. Liberation (kaivalya) arises when puruṣa discerns its absolute distinction from prakṛti, a discriminative knowledge (viveka‑jñāna) that halts the cycle of saṃsāra.

The Yoga school, systematised in Patañjali’s Yoga‑sūtras (c. 2nd century CE), adopts the Sāṃkhya metaphysics but adds a practical discipline (sādhana) to achieve the same discriminative insight. The eight‑limbed path (aṣṭāṅga‑yoga) — yama, niyama, āsana, prāṇāyāma, pratyāhāra, dhāraṇā, dhyāna, samādhi — functions as a technology for stilling the fluctuations (citta‑vṛtti) of the mind, which are themselves modifications of prakṛti. The guṇas remain the explanatory framework for mental states: a predominance of sattva yields clarity and calm; rajas produces restlessness; tamas yields dullness. The yogin’s progress is measured by the gradual predominance of sattva and the eventual transcendence of all three guṇas in nirbīja‑samādhi.

Commentarial literature sharpens the synthesis. Vyāsa’s Yoga‑bhāṣya (c. 5th century CE) explicitly identifies the Sāṃkhya tattvas as the ontological substrate of the yogic process, while Vācaspati Miśra’s Tattva‑vaiśāradī (9th century) reconciles the plural puruṣas of Sāṃkhya with the singular īśvara of Yoga (the special puruṣa untouched by kleśas). Later Advaita Vedāntins (e.g., Śaṅkara) critique the dualism, arguing that the guṇa‑based evolution cannot account for the non‑dual Brahman; nevertheless, the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework remains the default metaphysical vocabulary for most classical darśanas, including the medical Āyurveda and the aesthetic theory of rasa.

Key exam‑relevant distinctions: (1) Sāṃkhya is atheistic (no creator), Yoga is theistic (īśvara as a model puruṣa); (2) Sāṃkhya liberation is purely intellectual, Yoga adds psychosomatic practice; (3) The guṇas are not ethical qualities but ontological constituents — sattva is not “good” but “transparent”. Misreading them as moral categories is a frequent trap.

The Development of Buddhist Logic: Dignāga, Dharmakīrti, and the Pramāṇa Tradition

Buddhist epistemology (pramāṇa‑vāda) reaches its systematic peak in the works of Dignāga (c. 480–540 CE) and Dharmakīrti (c. 600–660 CE). Dignāga’s Pramāṇa‑samuccaya reduces the valid means of knowledge to two: perception (pratyakṣa) and inference (anumāna). He defines perception as a non‑conceptual, non‑erroneous cognition arising from the contact of sense‑faculty and object, and he introduces the famous “exclusion of the other” (anyāpoha) theory of universals: a concept denotes an object by excluding all non‑objects, thereby avoiding the Nyāya commitment to real universals (jāti).

Dharmakīrti expands this framework in the Pramāṇa‑vārttika and Nyāya‑bindu. He refines the definition of perception to include yogic perception (yogi‑pratyakṣa) — a direct, non‑conceptual cognition attained in meditative absorption — and he develops a sophisticated theory of svārthānumāna (inference for oneself) and parārthānumāna (inference for others). His “three‑fold criterion” (trairūpya) for a valid inferential sign (hetu) — presence in the subject, presence in similar cases, absence in dissimilar cases — becomes the standard for logical debate across Indian schools. Dharmakīrti also argues for momentariness (kṣaṇikatva) as a metaphysical corollary of perception: only momentary particulars can be directly perceived; enduring substances are conceptual constructions.

The Buddhist logicians engage in sustained polemics with Nyāya (especially Uddyotakara and Vātsyāyana) and Mīmāṃsā. Nyāya defends the reality of universals and a substantive self (ātman), while Dharmakīrti’s anātman stance uses the anyāpoha mechanism to explain language without positing eternal entities. The debate over the svabhāva (intrinsic nature) of the inferential sign influences later Navya‑Nyāya’s technical notion of avacchedaka (delimiter). In Tibet, Dharmakīrti’s texts become the core of the rigs‑pa (logic) curriculum, shaping the Gelug monastic education system.

For examination purposes, remember: (1) Dignāga = two pramāṇas, anyāpoha; (2) Dharmakīrti = three‑fold hetu, momentariness, yogic perception; (3) The Buddhist rejection of ātman is epistemologically grounded, not merely doctrinal. A common error is to conflate Dignāga’s anyāpoha with the later Navya‑Nyāya avacchedaka; they operate in opposite ontological directions.

Worked Example: Identifying the Hetu in a Dharmakīrtian Inference
A question states: “In the inference ‘Sound is impermanent because it is produced’, identify the hetu, the pakṣa, the sādhya, and verify the three‑fold criterion.”
Step 1: Pakṣa (subject) = sound. Step 2: Sādhya (probandum) = impermanence. Step 3: Hetu (reason) = being produced (utpanna). Step 4: Check trairūpya: (i) Hetu present in pakṣa — sound is produced . (ii) Hetu present in sapakṣa (similar cases) — all produced things (e.g., pot, cloud) are impermanent . (iii) Hetu absent in vipakṣa (dissimilar cases) — unproduced things (e.g., space, nirvāṇa) are not impermanent . All three conditions satisfied; the inference is valid.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the “Buddhist theory of universals”, answer “anyāpoha (exclusion of the other)” and contrast it with Nyāya’s “jāti (real universal)”. If the prompt mentions “three‑fold criterion”, immediately write the trairūpya formula; it scores full marks for recall and application.

The Vaiśeṣika Atomic Theory and its Debate with Nyāya on Categories

The Vaiśeṣika school, founded by Kaṇāda (c. 2nd century BCE) and systematised in the Vaiśeṣika‑sūtra, proposes a pluralistic ontology of seven categories (padārtha): substance (dravya), quality (guṇa), action (karman), generality (sāmānya), particularity (viśeṣa), inherence (samavāya), and non‑existence (abhāva). Its most distinctive contribution is the atomic theory: the four material substances — earth, water, fire, air — are composed of indivisible, eternal atoms (paramāṇu) that combine in dyads (dvyaṇuka) and triads (tryaṇuka) to form perceptible objects. Atoms possess specific qualities (colour, taste, smell, touch) that determine the macroscopic properties of the composites.

Nyāya, while accepting the Vaiśeṣika categories, reorganises them into sixteen padārthas (including pramāṇa, prameya, saṃśaya, etc.) and provides a rigorous logical apparatus for debate. The two schools converge in the classical period (c. 5th–10th century CE) into the “Nyāya‑Vaiśeṣika” synthesis, but their internal debates remain instructive. A central dispute concerns the status of sāmānya (universal). Vaiśeṣika treats universals as real, eternal entities inhering in particulars via samavāya. Nyāya, especially in the Navya‑Nyāya phase, refines this by distinguishing jāti (natural kind) from upādhi (imposed property) and by introducing the technical delimiter avacchedaka to avoid infinite regress in the relation between universal and particular.

Another flashpoint is the nature of abhāva (absence). Vaiśeṣika enumerates four types — prior non‑existence (prāgabhāva), posterior non‑existence (dhvamsābhāva), absolute non‑existence (atyantābhāva), and mutual non‑existence (anyonyābhāva). Nyāya accepts the taxonomy but argues that absence is a cognition‑dependent entity (pratiyogin) rather than an independent ontological category. This epistemological shift anticipates the Buddhist anyāpoha strategy.

Examination focus: (1) List the seven Vaiśeṣika padārthas and the four material atoms; (2) Explain the dyad‑triad composition; (3) Contrast Vaiśeṣika’s realist universals with Nyāya’s avacchedaka refinement; (4) Note that the atomic theory is not a physical theory in the modern sense but a metaphysical account of compositionality. A frequent trap is to attribute the “four elements” to Greek philosophy; the Indian atoms are qualitatively distinct and eternal.

The Role of the Haridāsa Movement in Kannada Bhakti Literature and Dvaita Vedānta

The Haridāsa (servant of Hari) movement flourishes in Karnataka from the 13th century onward, producing a vast corpus of devotional songs (kīrtanas, suladis, ugabhogas) in Kannada. Its most celebrated figures — Purandaradāsa (1484–1564), Kanakadāsa (1509–1609), and Vijayadāsa (1682–1755) — compose in simple metres (e.g., caupadi, sangatya) set to classical rāgas, making philosophical content accessible to lay audiences. Unlike the Vārkarī tradition of Maharashtra, which centres on Viṭṭhala of Paṇḍharpur, the Haridāsas explicitly propagate the Dvaita Vedānta of Madhvācārya (1238–1317), emphasising the eternal distinction between the supreme Viṣṇu (Īśvara) and the individual souls (jīva), graded by intrinsic merit (tāratamya).

Purandaradāsa, often called the “father of Carnatic music”, systematises the pedagogical framework of sarali‑varisai, janta‑varisai, and alankāra while embedding Dvaita doctrines: the reality of the world, the hierarchy of souls, and the necessity of bhakti combined with karma‑yoga for liberation. Kanakadāsa, a shepherd‑turned‑saint, uses autobiographical narratives to illustrate the Dvaita claim that even a low‑born devotee can attain the highest grade of devotion (uttama‑bhakti) through grace. His Nāla‑caritre and Haribhaktisāra juxtapose the futility of ritualistic caste pride with the efficacy of sincere surrender.

The movement’s institutional backbone is the maṭha network of the Dvaita monasteries (e.g., Uttarādi Maṭha, Vyasarāja Maṭha). These centres sponsor annual festivals (aradhane) where Haridāsa compositions are performed, ensuring oral transmission. Manuscript colophons reveal a scribal culture that standardises the textual corpus while allowing regional melodic variations. In the 19th century, the printing press (e.g., the Mysore Government Press) produces critical editions, enabling the movement’s entry into modern curricula.

Key points for the exam: (1) Haridāsa literature = vernacular vehicle for Dvaita metaphysics; (2) Purandaradāsa’s musical pedagogy is inseparable from his theological agenda; (3) The movement’s social base (merchant, artisan, shepherd) differs from the Vārkarī’s tailor‑potter composition, a distinction examiners test via “social composition” traps; (4) The concept of tāratamya is ontological, not epistemic — souls are eternally graded, not temporarily deluded.

The Transmission of Indian Philosophical Texts to China: The Silk Road and the Translation of Buddhist Sūtras

From the 1st century CE onward, the overland Silk Road and maritime routes facilitate the movement of Buddhist texts from India to China. The earliest translations, attributed to the Parthian monk An Shigao (c. 148–180 CE), render Hīnayāna vinaya and āgama texts into Chinese. The Kushan emperor Kaniṣka’s patronage (c. 127–150 CE) of the Fourth Buddhist Council in Kashmir produces a Sanskrit canon that becomes a source for later Chinese translators.

The most prolific phase occurs under the Eastern Han and the Six Dynasties (2nd–6th centuries). Kumārajīva (344–413 CE), a Kuchean scholar captured in Kucha and brought to Chang’an, leads a translation bureau that produces polished Chinese versions of the Madhyamaka‑śāstra, the Prajñāpāramitā sūtras, and the Vimalakīrti‑nirdeśa. His team’s “meaning‑for‑meaning” (yi‑yi) method contrasts with the earlier “word‑for‑word” (zhi‑zhi) approach, setting a philological standard.

In the Tang dynasty (618–907 CE), Xuanzang (602–664 CE) undertakes a perilous pilgrimage to India (629–645 CE), studies at Nālandā under Śīlabhadra, and returns with over 600 Sanskrit manuscripts. His translation academy at the Great Wild Goose Pagoda produces the Cheng‑weishi‑lun (Yogācāra‑bhūmi) and the Mahāprajñāpāramitā‑śāstra, establishing the Faxiang (Dharma‑characteristics) school in China. Simultaneously, the Esoteric (Mantrayāna) texts arrive via the maritime route; Śubhakarasiṃha, Vajrabodhi, and Amoghavajra translate the Mahāvairocana‑sūtra and the Tattvasaṃgraha‑tantra, seeding the Zhenyan (True Word) tradition.

The transmission is not a passive copy; Chinese scholars reinterpret Indian concepts through indigenous categories. The Madhyamaka “two truths” (satya‑dvaya) becomes the “ultimate truth” (zhēndì) and “conventional truth” (sú​dì) in the Sanlun school; the Yogācāra “store‑consciousness” (ālaya‑vijñāna) is mapped onto the Confucian “mind‑heart” (xīn) discourse. Tibetan translation later (8th–11th centuries) follows a more literal Sanskrit‑Tibetan lexicon, preserving technical terms that Chinese renderings had domesticated.

Examination essentials: (1) Identify the three major translation waves — Han (An Shigao, Lokakṣema), Six Dynasties (Kumārajīva), Tang (Xuanzang, Esoteric masters); (2) Distinguish the “word‑for‑word” vs. “meaning‑for‑meaning” methods; (3) Know the key texts each translator rendered; (4) Recognise that Chinese schools (Sanlun, Tiantai, Huayan, Chan, Faxiang, Zhenyan) are doctrinal adaptations, not mere replicas. A common trap is to attribute the “Heart Sūtra” translation solely to Xuanzang; the earliest Chinese version is by Kumārajīva.

  • Conflating the Sāṃkhya guṇas with ethical qualities rather than ontological constituents.
  • Attributing Dignāga’s anyāpoha to Nyāya’s avacchedaka or vice‑versa.
  • Treating Vaiśeṣika atoms as modern physical particles instead of metaphysical simples.
  • Assuming the Haridāsa movement is socially identical to the Vārkarī tradition.
  • Believing Xuanzang was the first to translate the Prajñāpāramitā into Chinese.
  • Reading the Dvaita tāratamya as a temporary hierarchy removable by knowledge.
  • Confusing the “word‑for‑word” translation method with the later Tibetan literalist approach.
These sections provide a deeper analytical framework for the study of Indian literature, religion, and philosophy.

Gaudapada’s Māṇḍūkya Kārikā and the Advaita–Madhyamaka Dialogue

The Māṇḍūkya Kārikā, attributed to Gauḍapāda (c. 6th–7th century CE), is the earliest systematic exposition of non‑dual (advaita) metaphysics in Sanskrit. Its four chapters (prakaraṇas) comment on the Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad, but the work’s philosophical architecture shows a deliberate engagement with Madhyamaka Buddhist thought, particularly the doctrine of two truths (satya‑dvaya) and the negation of intrinsic nature (svabhāva). Gauḍapāda adopts the Madhyamaka method of reductio ad absurdum (prasaṅga) to demonstrate that the waking, dreaming, and deep‑sleep states are all mithyā — empirically real but ontologically dependent — while the fourth state (turīya) alone is non‑dual consciousness (prajñāna).

Scholars note three points of convergence: (1) the use of the “neither existent nor non‑existent” (na bhāva nābhāva) formula to describe phenomenal reality; (2) the rejection of causation (kāraṇa) as an ultimate principle, echoing Nāgārjuna’s critique of pratītyasamutpāda as a conventional construct; (3) the deployment of the “ajātivāda” (non‑origination) argument, which Gauḍapāda extends to assert that the world never truly originates, a stance that anticipates Śaṅkara’s later vivarta‑vāda. The divergence lies in the ontological status granted to the witness‑consciousness (sākṣin). For Madhyamaka, even the cognizing subject is empty; for Gauḍapāda, the self‑luminous ātman remains the unchanging substrate, a position that becomes the cornerstone of Advaita Vedānta.

The Kārikā’s influence is traceable in Śaṅkara’s Brahmasūtra‑bhāṣya, where the latter cites Gauḍapāda as “the teacher of my teacher” (guror gurur). The text also shaped the later Vivaraṇa and Bhāmatī sub‑schools, which debated whether the world’s appearance is a positive superimposition (adhyāsa) or a mere absence of knowledge. In examination terms, the Kārikā serves as a bridge text: it demonstrates how a Brahmanical thinker appropriated Buddhist dialectic while re‑asserting a permanent self, a pattern that recurs in later Vedānta‑Buddhist polemics.

The Development of Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit in Mahāyāna Sūtra Transmission

Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit (BHS) is a register of Sanskrit that emerged in the early centuries CE as Mahāyāna communities composed and transmitted sūtras in a language that retained Prakrit phonology, morphology, and lexical items while conforming loosely to Pāṇinian norms. The term, coined by Franklin Edgerton, describes texts such as the Lalitavistara, the Mahāvastu, and the Saddharmapuṇḍarīka (Lotus Sūtra) in their Sanskrit recensions. BHS is not a single dialect but a spectrum: at one end, near‑classical Sanskrit with occasional Prākritisms (e.g., “buddha” for “buddhaḥ”); at the other, heavily Prākritised verses preserving Magadhi or Śauraseni forms.

The motivation for BHS was twofold. First, it allowed Mahāyāna authors to claim the prestige of Sanskrit — the language of Brahmanical scholarship — while remaining intelligible to monastic audiences accustomed to Prakrit recitation. Second, it facilitated the translation project into Chinese: translators such as Lokakṣema (2nd century CE) and Kumārajīva (4th–5th century CE) worked from BHS manuscripts, and their renderings preserve traces of the hybrid grammar (e.g., the use of the instrumental singular “-ena” where classical Sanskrit would require “-ena” but with a Prakrit stem). The BHS corpus also shows a systematic replacement of Vedic technical terms with Buddhist equivalents: “dharma” for “ṛta”, “bodhi” for “mokṣa”, and “śūnyatā” for “nirodha”.

From a philological standpoint, BHS texts are crucial for reconstructing the evolution of Middle Indo‑Aryan. They exhibit the loss of the dual number, the merger of the aorist and perfect, and the spread of the periphrastic future — features that later appear in Apabhraṃśa and early New Indo‑Aryan. In the context of the UPSC syllabus, BHS illustrates the mechanism of “Sanskritisation” of Buddhist literature: a deliberate linguistic strategy that enabled doctrinal diffusion across linguistic zones without abandoning the vernacular roots of the sangha.

Comparative Textual Analysis
How does the use of Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit in the Lotus Sūtra reflect the doctrinal adaptation of Mahāyāna concepts for a Sanskrit‑literate audience?
The Lotus Sūtra’s Sanskrit recension employs BHS forms such as “buddha‑kṣetra” (instead of classical “buddha‑kṣetram”) and retains Prakrit‑style sandhi in verses (e.g., “sarva‑dharma‑pravṛttaye”). These features signal that the text was composed for recitation in monastic assemblies where Prakrit pronunciation prevailed, while the Sanskrit veneer granted it authority in debates with Brahmanical opponents. Doctrinally, the hybrid language mirrors the sūtra’s central claim — that the one vehicle (ekayāna) subsumes the three vehicles — by presenting a linguistic “one vehicle” that bridges the vernacular and the classical. Examiners reward answers that link specific morphological traits (loss of dual, instrumental‑case extensions) to the sociolinguistic strategy of Mahāyāna expansion.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “linguistic evidence of Buddhist Sanskritisation”, cite two concrete BHS features (e.g., instrumental singular “-ena” on Prakrit stems, retention of Prakrit vowel gradation) and connect each to a transmission context (oral recitation, Chinese translation). Avoid vague statements like “it mixed Sanskrit and Prakrit”.

The Siddha Tradition in Tamil Medical and Alchemical Literature

The Tamil Siddha (cittar) tradition, flourishing from the 8th century CE onward, produced a corpus of medical, alchemical, and yogic texts composed in Tamil verse (often in the viruttam metre) and, in later periods, in a mixed Tamil‑Sanskrit register. Key figures — Tirumūlar (Tirumandiram), Patañjali (identified with the author of the Yoga Sūtras in Tamil hagiography), and the eighteen canonical Siddhars such as Agastya, Bogar, and Korakkar — are credited with works on rasa‑śāstra (mercurial alchemy), kāya‑kalpa (rejuvenation), and muṇi‑vidyā (pulse diagnosis). The texts emphasise the transmutation of base metals into gold as a metaphor for the transformation of the body into a “divine body” (divya‑deha) through mercury (rasa) and sulfur (gandhaka) preparations.

Unlike the north Indian Ayurvedic samhitās, which systematise humoral theory (tridoṣa) in Sanskrit prose, Siddha literature embeds its pharmacology within a devotional framework: the preparation of medicines is accompanied by mantra recitation, and the laboratory (rasa‑śālā) is treated as a sacred space. The “Siddha Pharmacopoeia” (e.g., the “Cittar Māṇi” and “Agastya Kāvyam”) lists over 300 mineral, herbal, and animal substances, many of which (e.g., “nava‑pāṣāṇa”, “vanga‑bhasma”) have no direct counterpart in the Caraka or Suśruta texts. Manuscript evidence from the Tamil Nadu State Archives shows that these works were copied in palm‑leaf bundles with colophons recording the guru‑śiṣya lineage, indicating an oral‑written transmission parallel to the Vedic tradition.

In the early modern period, the Siddha corpus interacted with Unani medicine introduced by the Delhi Sultanate and later the Mughal court. The 17th‑century text “Rasa‑ratnākara” (attributed to the Siddha Kāñcī Śivācārya) incorporates Persian technical terms such as “āb‑e‑ḥayāt” (elixir of life) and “ḥabb” (pill), demonstrating a two‑way lexical exchange. For the examination, the Siddha tradition exemplifies a regional knowledge system that maintains doctrinal autonomy while selectively absorbing external scientific vocabulary — a pattern also visible in the later Sanskrit astronomical texts that adopt Persian terminology.

The Nighaṇṭu Tradition and the Classification of Vedic Vocabulary

The Nighaṇṭu (lexicon) tradition constitutes a distinct strand of Sanskrit lexicography that predates and runs parallel to the more famous Amarakośa. The earliest extant Nighaṇṭu is the “Nighaṇṭu” attributed to the Vedic sage Yāska (c. 5th century BCE), which serves as a glossary of difficult Vedic words arranged by semantic categories (e.g., deities, metres, sacrificial implements). Yāska’s Nirukta, a commentary on the Nighaṇṭu, introduces etymological analysis (nirvacana) as a method for recovering the “original meaning” (artha) of Vedic terms, establishing a hermeneutic that later grammarians such as Pāṇini and Kātyāyana would reference.

In the post‑Vedic period, the Nighaṇṭu genre expands to cover medical, botanical, and technical vocabularies. The “Dhanvantari Nighaṇṭu” (c. 10th century CE) classifies medicinal substances (dravya) by rasa (taste), vīrya (potency), and vipāka (post‑digestive effect), mirroring the Ayurvedic tridoṣa framework. The “Rāja Nighaṇṭu” (12th century CE) adds a botanical taxonomy based on habitat (vana, audbhida, etc.) and includes synonyms in Prakrit and Apabhraṃśa, reflecting the multilingual milieu of medieval pharmacology. These works differ from the Amarakośa’s purely synonymic arrangement by embedding each entry in a doctrinal or practical system, making them functional reference tools for physicians and ritualists alike.

The transmission of Nighaṇṭus followed the same manuscript culture as the Vedic texts: palm‑leaf copies with colophons naming the scribe, the patron, and the guru‑paramparā. In the 19th century, colonial scholars such as Horace Hayman Wilson and Theodor Aufrecht edited and printed critical editions, which became the basis for modern Ayurvedic curricula. For the aspirant, the Nighaṇṭu tradition illustrates how lexical works served as “knowledge infrastructures” that stabilised technical terminology across generations and facilitated the cross‑regional diffusion of scientific concepts.

The Adoption of Persian Technical Vocabulary in Sanskrit Astronomical Works of the Mughal Era

During the 16th–17th centuries, Sanskrit astronomers (jyotiṣīs) attached to the Mughal court produced a series of texts — notably the “Zīj‑i‑Muḥammad‑Shāhī” (Sanskrit: “Muḥammad‑Shāhī‑Zīj”) and the “Siddhānta‑śiromaṇi‑ṭīkā” of Nṛsiṃha Daivajña — that incorporate Persian and Arabic technical terms alongside traditional Sanskrit vocabulary. Terms such as “jayb” (sine), “koti” (cosine), “usṭurlāb” (astrolabe), and “zīj” (astronomical table) appear in Sanskritised forms (jīva, koṭi, astrolābha, jīja) and are defined using the indigenous pāṭa‑gaṇita (mathematical) framework.

This lexical borrowing was not wholesale translation but a selective adaptation: the Sanskrit authors retained the computational algorithms of the Siddhāntic tradition (e.g., the kuṭṭaka method for solving indeterminate equations) while adopting the Persian convention of tabulating planetary positions in degrees and minutes (a sexagesimal system) rather than the traditional nakṣatra‑based zodiac. The “Zīj‑i‑Muḥammad‑Shāhī” includes a preface in Persian explaining the project’s patronage by Emperor Muḥammad Shāh (r. 1719–1748) and a Sanskrit colophon asserting the text’s conformity to the “ancient science of the ṛṣis”. This bilingual framing reflects a strategic positioning: the work claims continuity with the Vedic jyotiṣa lineage while demonstrating competence in the “new” Islamic astronomy favoured at court.

Manuscript evidence from the Royal Library of Jaipur and the British Library shows that these texts circulated in both Devanāgarī and Nastaʿlīq scripts, and that marginal glosses often provide Persian equivalents for Sanskrit technical terms. The phenomenon illustrates a broader pattern in early modern Indian intellectual history: the selective incorporation of foreign terminology into a living Sanskrit scientific tradition without surrendering its methodological core. In examination answers, candidates should distinguish this “technical bilingualism” from the earlier, more wholesale translation movements of the Abbasid period.

  • Confusing Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit with classical Sanskrit and missing the Prakrit morphological markers that signal Mahāyāna compositional strategy.
  • Attributing the ajātivāda doctrine solely to Śaṅkara while overlooking Gauḍapāda’s prior formulation in the Māṇḍūkya Kārikā.
  • Treating the Siddha alchemical corpus as a mere offshoot of Ayurveda, ignoring its distinct devotional‑laboratory framework and Tamil linguistic base.
  • Assuming the Nighaṇṭu tradition is synonymous with the Amarakośa, thereby missing the semantic‑category organisation and etymological method of Yāska’s Nirukta.
  • Overstating Persian influence by claiming Sanskrit astronomy was “replaced” by Islamic tables, rather than recognising the selective lexical adoption within an indigenous computational framework.
  • Neglecting the role of manuscript colophons in establishing transmission lineages for both Siddha and Nighaṇṭu texts, a detail examiners use to test source‑criticism skills.
  • Conflating the “zīj” genre with the earlier Siddhānta texts, missing the shift from nakṣatra‑based to degree‑minute tabulation that marks the Mughal‑era synthesis.
